Its sounds are the game's, rendered by `m4a_render.py` (`slotSound.ts`): coins in, the stop button, the payout's pins, the explosion and the menu; the fanfares are MUS_SLOTS_WIN, MUS_SLOTS_JACKPOT (Bonuses) and MUS_TOO_BAD, and Reel Time's lottery plays MUS_ROULETTE in a loop (its loop alone, without the intro), the Game Corner's music paused meanwhile and played on where it was after (the game starts it over).

**Voltorb Flip** (`/extras/voltorb-flip`, `VoltorbFlip.tsx`): HeartGold and SoulSilver's card game from the Goldenrod Game Corner, ported frame by frame from the [pret/pokeheartgold](https://github.com/pret/pokeheartgold) decompilation's `src/voltorb_flip/` (the rules `voltorb-flip.ts`, the screen `VoltorbFlipScreen.tsx`). A 5×5 board of face-down cards, each a 1, 2, 3 or Voltorb, with each row's and column's point total and Voltorb count beside it: the first card flipped gives its coins, every card after multiplies them; a Voltorb loses them all ("Oh no! You get 0 Coins!"), Quit keeps them, and finding every 2 and 3 clears the board ("Game clear!"). The level (1 to 8) is the game's: each level's 10 boards (their Voltorbs, 2s and 3s, and how many multipliers may be free of risk, laid out again until few enough are), the next level from the last round (won: up a level; otherwise as many levels as cards it flipped, at most its own) and Lv. 8 only after five rounds in a row of 8 or more cards and no loss from Lv. 5 up; the level starts at 1 each time the game is entered, as the game's does. Everything is the game's: the menu (Play, Game Info, Quit), Game Info's How to Play, Hint! and About Memos screens, the memo (marks for Voltorb, 1, 2 and 3, by touch or the D-pad), "Is this what you're expecting?!" when 3 in 4 of the cards left in the card's row or column are Voltorbs, the coins counted up a coin a frame (a tap counts them at once), the reveal and the board turned back over column by column; its graphics (the screens, tiles and sprites, drawn from the decompilation's NSCR, NCER and NANR files) and its sounds and fanfares, rendered from HeartGold's sound archive (`sdat.py`, `nds_render.py`). The bottom screen is the touch screen; the keyboard plays the buttons (the arrows the D-pad, Space or Z the A button, X or Backspace the B button, M or C the X button). The coins go into the account's Coin Case, the Game Corner's (`User.coins`, at most 9999, so the game's "You've gathered 50,000 Coins" says 9,999 and it won't play while the Coin Case is full). The API makes each board and keeps the session (`extras.voltorbRound`: its last 5 rounds and the board in play, `User.voltorbFlip`), and settles each round against the board (`extras.voltorbSettle`: the cards flipped in order and how it ended, checked by `settleRound`, the coins added with the account's row locked); the board is sent to the browser, which draws it. Auto play plays HeartGold and SoulSilver's Goldenrod Game Corner theme (the game keeps the Game Corner's music on), in either soundtrack. The text uses the site's Emerald font and plain window frames, menu buttons and YES/NO, not the DS's.

## Colour themes

The Settings page's Theme section (`ThemePicker`) changes the app's colours: Slate (the original look) and 28 themes from the games, at least 9 a generation, drawn from the starters (Bulbasaur, Charmander, Squirtle; Chikorita, Cyndaquil, Totodile; Treecko, Torchic, Mudkip, and Pikachu), the versions (Red, Blue, Gold, Silver, Crystal, Ruby, Sapphire, Emerald), the original Game Boy's green screen and places (Viridian Forest, Lavender Town, Seafoam Islands, Ecruteak City, Ilex Forest, Lake of Rage, Route 113, Sootopolis City, Fallarbor Town). It's remembered in the browser (`poke-tcg.theme`), not the account.

How it works: the app's chrome is Tailwind's slate (backgrounds, panels, rings, text, the scroll bars: a 4px rounded thumb in slate-600 with no track, 12px wide to grab and at least 48px long, `index.css`) with a yellow accent (the page lit in the menu, selection rings, ₱), and Tailwind v4 reads each colour class from a CSS variable. A theme (`themes.ts`) is a hue and a tint for the neutrals and a hue for the accent; `themeVars` makes them into `--color-slate-50`…`950` and `--color-yellow-50`…`400` in OKLCH on Tailwind's own lightness steps (so text stays as readable as on slate), the chroma cut to what sRGB shows, and an accent shade going up to 0.2 darker where its hue can't be vivid that light (a red as light as yellow-300 would be nearly white). `useColorTheme` sets them on `<html>`; Slate sets none. Yellow 500 and darker aren't themed: they mean things in play (Paralyzed, Lightning, the gym leader's icon), and the classic coin's gold face is amber. The playmats are drawn on a canvas, which CSS variables don't reach, so `Playmat` takes their colours from `playmatColors` and redraws when the theme changes: the faint lines are the theme's slate-400, and a click's light a bright version of the theme's hue (as Slate's sky blue is of its slate), fainter for the barely tinted themes (Silver, Route 113). New chrome colours should be slate classes, yellow 50–400 for the accent, or `var(--color-slate-…)` in CSS and inline styles (as the music player's bar and the deck piles' edges do), never a fixed hex, so the themes reach them.


Light themes (agreed 2026-10-23; `light` on a theme): the neutral and accent shades turned round (`LIGHT_NEUTRAL`, `LIGHT_ACCENT` in `themes.ts`), so the page (slate-950) is a subdued white, the text (slate-50 to 200) near black and the accent dark enough to read as text, every pair the dark themes use reading the other way up; the browser's own controls go light too (`color-scheme`, `useColorTheme`), and the playmat's lines darker. Text that must stay light on a slate background is `text-slate-50` (`hover:text-slate-50`), never `text-white`, so a light theme turns it dark. The first is **Start Menu**, beside Slate in the System themes group (the first of the picker's groups, before the generations'): FireRed's start menu, near-black text on a soft white, its frame's blue-grey the accent.
## Account settings

The Settings page's choices follow the account, not just the browser (agreed 2026-10-23): the colour theme, the font, skipping coin flip animations, the CPU's played cards shown (Pokémon, Energy), End turn's and attacks' reminders, the music's auto play and soundtrack, and the EXP bar and Pokédollars in the theme's colour (`ACCOUNT_SETTINGS` in `packages/shared/src/settings.ts`; the account name was already the account's). They're kept in `User.settings` (JSON, migration `20261107090000_account_settings`), each as the web app's own value in a string, through the `settings` router (`apps/api/src/routers/settings.ts`: `get`, and `save`, which merges the ones changed; null removes one). Each setting's store still keeps it in localStorage, so it applies at once on a page load (the theme and font before the first render); once signed in, `AccountSettingsSync` loads the account's and `utils/accountSettings.ts` brings each store in line: a value the account has replaces the browser's (unless it was changed in this browser before the account's arrived), and one the browser has saved but the account hasn't is saved to it (so the first browser's choices carry over). Every change is saved to the account a moment later (several together). The music player's other state (the song, volume, shuffle, its panel) stays in the browser.

## Music

A small music player is on every page once signed in (`MusicProvider` in `MusicPlayer.tsx`, mounted with the root layout so music plays on from page to page). Its bar (`MusicControls`) sits at the right end of the top bar on a big screen, before Sign out, battles included, its panel opening under it; on a phone it's in the site's ☰ menu instead, and on the phone battle screen in its ☰ menu, beside Concede, so it never covers the hand. There the panel opens at the bottom of the screen and opening it closes the menu. The bar has play/pause and the song playing (while nothing plays, just play and the note, the song in the note's tooltip; the user's); clicking the song (or the note) pops out the panel with the track controls from [react-h5-audio-player](https://github.com/lhz516/react-h5-audio-player) (seek bar, previous/next, styled in `index.css`), a volume slider whose top is half the audio's full volume (`MAX_VOLUME`), a repeat button (our own, the size of the others: grey when off, white when on; remembered with the player's settings) and a shuffle button, a generation filter (All, Gen 1–5) and the song list: game by game, each game's music, then its battle music. Each song has a play button on its left that starts it at once; clicking the song itself only selects (highlights) it, leaving the song playing and the controls as they are. When one ends the next one in the list plays (a random one with shuffle). The panel, the filter, shuffle, the volume and the last song are remembered in the browser (`poke-tcg.music`); nothing plays until you press play. Clicks on the player don't cancel a move in progress (`data-keep-action`).

**Auto play** (agreed 2026-10-23; on by default, with the Remastered soundtrack, agreed 2026-10-23; switched on by the button beside shuffle or on the Settings page, remembered with the player's settings) makes the music follow the game, looping the song that suits it: on a run's map, the route's theme (as the games play it on that map: the bike's or Surf's on a route ridden or surfed), then the town's over the last 3 steps before the gym (`TOWN_STEPS`), the Pokémon Gym theme inside the gym, and the Pokémon League's (Kanto's and Hoenn's Victory Road) in the Elite Four's and Champion's rooms; in a battle, once its game is on, a trainer's (Gym Trainers' and Ace Trainers' too) one of the trainer battle, wild Pokémon and rival themes at random, a gym leader's the Gym Leader theme, the Elite Four's theirs (the Gym Leader theme in Kanto and Johto, as the games have it) and the Champion's theirs; in a Pokémon Center (its encounter, or the Gym Challenge's center) the Pokémon Center theme, and when Nurse Joy heals, the Pokémon Healed jingle and then straight back to the song it interrupted, where it was. A song auto play leaves is remembered where it was and picks up from there when it comes back (the route's after a battle), but every battle starts its song from the beginning (the cue's `fromStart`). Songs fade into each other (`FADE_MS`, 1 second out, then 1 second in once the next is playing; timed with an interval so a hidden tab still switches), list changes and jingles too, except battle music (the user's): a battle's song cuts in at once, at full volume, from the click that chooses the battle (or accepts an Ace Trainer; useRun's `spotted`), the cue's `cut`, and keeps playing into its game (its cue keyed by the run's id and its count of battles fought, so the same song carries on); leaving a battle fades back to the route's song. The Viridian City run plays Rocket Hideout on the Power Plant and over its town steps, in both soundtracks (the user's). A beaten run plays its region's victory theme once (`victoryMusic`: Victory! (Gym Leader), or the Hall of Fame for Red/Blue, whose victory jingles are under a minute), then shuffles the soundtrack album's other music, its battle music left out (the cue's `then`), until another cue or a song picked from the list. Training battles play their opponent's theme (a boss's region; a trainer's Kanto's); other pages leave the song playing as it is. The Settings page picks the soundtrack: **Original** (Red/Blue, Gold/Silver, Ruby & Sapphire) or **Remastered** (FireRed & LeafGreen, HeartGold & SoulSilver, Omega Ruby & Alpha Sapphire). Which song each map and town plays comes from the games' own map data (the pret decompilations: pokered, pokefirered, pokecrystal, pokeheartgold, pokeemerald; `REGION_MUSIC` in `packages/campaign/src/music.ts`, the shared themes noted there). Auto play's few songs under a minute (the healing jingles, FireRed's Route 1 theme, Ruby & Sapphire's Fortree City, and the Idle Adventure's: Oak's lab's in both albums and Red's Route 24 theme; the user's: a song a place needs is added however short) are `hidden` in `tracks.json`: left out of the song list.

The songs are streamed straight from [KHInsider](https://downloads.khinsider.com) (the MP3 links of its file host, listed in `apps/web/public/music/tracks.json`, fetched the first time the player is used), one album per game, each game in the generation of the region it's set in rather than the one it came out in (remakes listed after their originals): Red, Green, Blue & Yellow and FireRed & LeafGreen (Super Music Collection, without its bonus arrangements) in Gen 1; Gold, Silver & Crystal and HeartGold & SoulSilver in Gen 2; Ruby & Sapphire (Music Super Complete), Emerald (its game rip, without the hidden tracks from other games) and Omega Ruby & Alpha Sapphire (Super Music Complete, discs 1–4, without discs 5–6, the original Ruby & Sapphire soundtrack) in Gen 3; Diamond, Pearl & Platinum in Gen 4; Black & White (its titles are the album's, with Japanese place names, without its 2 bonus arrangements) and Black 2 & White 2 (Super Music Collection, without its Emerald and Platinum discs) in Gen 5. Only tracks of a minute or more are listed. A track is battle music when its title says so ("Battle!", "Final Battle!", "Decisive Battle!", "vs."). The list was built once with a script reading the album pages and each song page for its MP3 link; if KHInsider moves its files, the list has to be rebuilt.

## Single battles

The **Training** tab (`pages/SingleBattle.tsx`) plays one battle outside runs: nothing is won or lost. Pick:
- **Your deck:** one of your decks that can be played (the others are listed with why not), or the fixed Base Set test deck (`PLAYER_TEST_DECK` in `apps/api/src/test-decks.ts`). The deck for the next battle is shown large at the top, with its headline Pokémon and card split, and links to the Collection tab to edit it (`/decks?deck=<id>`) or build a new one (`/decks?deck=new`).
- **Opponent:** a random trainer from the run's map (`TRAINERS`), with a new random deck suiting their name each battle (`generateDeck` with `trainerFavorites`, as in runs), or a boss (any gym leader, the Elite Four or Champion Blue) with their own deck.
- **Difficulty:** Passive, Trainer (the default) or Gym leader, whatever the opponent.

The choices are remembered in the browser (`poke-tcg.single-battle`), so after a battle the Next battle button goes back to the tab with them still picked. `game.create` (`singleBattleInput` in `@poke-tcg/shared`) starts it; the opponent's name is kept in the game's state (`opponentName`) for the banner at the top of the game.

## How a move works

1. Client sends `game.act({ gameId, expectedVersion, action })`.
2. API validates the input with Zod, loads the game state, and runs `applyAction` from `@poke-tcg/game-engine`.
3. If the move is legal, the API runs the CPU (`runCpu`) until it's the player's move again.
4. If `expectedVersion` still matches, the API writes the new state and every action (player and CPU) to the `GameAction` log in one transaction.
5. The response is `frames`: the player's `getPlayerView` after their move and after each of the CPU's, with who moved and the action type. A move that ended a turn whose Pokémon Checkup flipped coins (Sleep, Burn, Char) is two frames (`moveFrames`): the board as the checkup began (`captureCheckup` in the engine, which `runCpu` uses too: only real moves, never the CPU's imagined ones), then the checkup as an `END_TURN`, so an attack and the Sleep it gives show before the flip to wake up. When a Pokémon Tool worked by itself in the checkup (a Berry: the log entry's `tool`, `logTool`), it's three: the move, the checkup as it ended (`captureCheckup`'s `end`, before the next turn begins), then the next turn, so the Tool's animation (`ToolPops`) plays between the attack and the turn's end (the user's). Raw state, which includes the CPU's hand, deck order and RNG, never leaves the server.
6. The web caches the last frame and plays the others back (`pages/Game.tsx`): each move's coin flips play first (1.9s each) while the board still shows the move before, so no result shows before its coin lands; then the move stays up for 0.9s. A click skips a move's flips, and with "Skip coin flip animations" on the Settings page (saved per browser) they don't play at all. Nothing can be clicked until the reply ends, coin flips included. Slots animate what changed (`Slot.tsx`): a Pokémon dropping in, a red shake for damage, green for healing, yellow for Energy, and the attacker lunging at the defender.

## The CPU opponent

Each difficulty has its own file in `packages/game-engine/src/cpu/`, with shared helpers in `shared.ts`. A game's `cpuDifficulty` picks one (missing = Gym leader), and the Debug tab can change it mid-game:

- **Passive** (`passive.ts`): benches Basics, evolves and attaches Energy to a Pokémon of the same type. It never attacks, retreats or plays Trainers.
- **Trainer** (`trainer.ts`), for the fights that aren't gym leaders: plays Bill and Professor Oak (same rules as the gym leader), benches and evolves, aims its Energy attachment like the gym leader, heals only its active (Potion at 20+, Super Potion at 40+ if it can still use its best attack), retreats or switches only when the active can't attack this turn or next and a benched Pokémon can, and attacks for the most damage even when that costs Energy or hurts itself. It also plays a short list of simple Trainers from each generation (`TRAINER_CPU_PLAYS`: draw, search, healing, Pokémon Tools, Energy from the discard pile) and special Energy (`TRAINER_CPU_ENERGY`: Potion, Full Heal, Rainbow and Recycle Energy, and Double Colorless), whenever the generic judgement below finds they leave a better position (agreed with the user: enough for its random decks, but weaker than the gym leader). It never uses Pokémon Powers or Stadiums, disrupts, boosts, or looks at the player's next turn. After a Knock Out it promotes the Pokémon with the most HP left.
- **Gym leader** (`gym-leader.ts`): the strongest, described below.

Neither the Trainer nor the gym leader CPU ever uses an attack that could lose it the game (the user's: not losing comes before any damage): each attack is played out on copies over every coin-flip pattern (`attackOptions`), and the Knock Outs it leaves are settled as the engine does (`resolveKnockouts`), so an attack that Knocks Out the attacker with no Bench left (Selfdestruct), or gives the player their last Prize cards, is dropped in any outcome (`loseChance`, `safe` in `shared.ts`); with nothing else it doesn't attack. One that wins the game too (both sides out of Pokémon or Prizes, the attacker's win) is kept.

The gym leader takes, on its turn, it takes the first move any of these steps wants, over and over, until it attacks or ends its turn:

1. **Draw and search**: Bill (keeping cards to draw). Computer Search for the next stage of a line in play, or a Basic if the Bench is empty, discarding Energy nothing can use, spare Trainers, Trainers it never plays and Pokémon it can't use (never anything else). Pokémon Trader swaps a Pokémon it can't use for one it wants. It only searches when the card could still be in its deck (counting the face-down prizes). Pokédex isn't played.
   Gym Heroes and Gym Challenge: Master Ball, Brock's Training Method and Erika's Maids (trading 2 cards it can't use) for the next stage of a line in play; Good Manners for a Basic when there's room and none in the hand; Erika (with 3 or fewer other cards and a deck over 8) and Misty's Wrath (deck over 10); Blaine's Last Resort; Secret Mission when 2 or more cards are of no use (it discards only those); Misty's Tears and Fervor when it has Water or Fire Pokémon and no Energy of that type in hand; Rocket's Secret Experiment when it wants a card from the deck.
2. **Develop**: bench Basics, Pokémon Breeder straight to Stage 2, Giovanni when it lets a Pokémon with Giovanni in its name evolve now, evolve (active first), Max Revive paid with 2 Energy cards nothing can use, and a Clefairy Doll only when the Bench is empty.
3. **Energy**: Rain Dance and the turn's attachment go to the active if they make a new attack usable, otherwise to the benched Pokémon they bring closest to attacking; Double Colorless only where two colorless symbols are open, and never Energy no attack can use. Energy Trans moves Grass Energy from the Bench to the active when that makes a stronger attack usable. Then Professor Oak, if the hand is down to 2 other cards (and the deck has more than 7). Blaine, when a Pokémon with Blaine in its name is 2 or more Energy from its strongest attack and the hand has 2 Fire Energy cards: both go to it.
4. **Heal**: Full Heal for a Special Condition; Super Potion on the active at 40+ damage if it can spare the Energy and still use its best attack, else the most damaged benched Pokémon at 40+; Potion at 20+ damage (active first); Pokémon Center for 60+ healed when the active isn't about to attack with Energy it would lose; Damage Swap moves damage counters off an active that would be Knocked Out. Gym sets: Celadon City Gym for a Special Condition on its Active Pokémon with Erika in its name, if it can spare an Energy card; Brock once 2 or more of its Pokémon are damaged; Erika's Kindness when it heals 30 or more of its own and more than the player's; Giovanni's Last Resort on a Pokémon with Giovanni in its name at half its HP or worse, with 2 or fewer other cards in hand.
5. **Disrupt**: Energy Removal on the player's active (else the Pokémon closest to its strongest attack); Super Energy Removal only when both Energy were going towards one attack, discarding its own Energy that's missed least; Gust of Wind for a Knock Out, else to strand a benched Pokémon with no Energy and a Retreat Cost of 2+. The Rocket's Trap against 3 or more cards in the player's hand; an owner's Stadium (Pewter, Cerulean, Celadon, Viridian, Vermilion or Cinnabar City Gym) when it has 2 or more of that owner's Pokémon in play and more than the player (`OWNER_STADIUMS`); Brock's Protection on its Pokémon with Brock in its name that has the most Energy (2 or more).
6. **Retreat or Switch** (Switch first): when the active can't attack this turn or next but a benched Pokémon can; when it would be Knocked Out next turn (and isn't Knocking Out the attacker first) and a benched Pokémon would survive; or when it still has a Special Condition. The benched Pokémon that attacks best comes in. The Retreat Cost is paid with the Energy it can best spare, basic before special, and never when nothing could attack afterwards.
7. **Boost**: any Pokémon Power that makes this turn's attack better without costing a Pokémon or prize (Energy Burn); PlusPower when +10 makes a Knock Out, then Misty (discarding 2 cards it can't use); Koga before a Pokémon with Koga in its name damages one that isn't Poisoned; Defender when −20 stops a Knock Out next turn, and Koga's Ninja Trick on an Active Pokémon with Koga in its name that would be Knocked Out.
8. **Tickling Machine**, only when it has no attack that does damage (tails would end its turn).
9. **Attack**: the cheapest attack that Knocks Out, otherwise the most damage. Attacks that hurt the attacker or discard its Energy only when they Knock Out or nothing else does damage.

After a Knock Out it promotes the benched Pokémon that attacks best (then the one with the most HP left).

Every other card the gym leader plays through the generic judgement (`cpu/evaluate.ts`, agreed with the user: hand-written rules first, this for every other Trainer, Pokémon Power, Stadium and special Energy). A move is tried on 4 copies of the state the CPU imagines: its own deck and Prize cards and the player's hand, deck and Prize cards reshuffled, and coin flips from fixed patterns (each flip heads in half of them), so it never peeks at a draw or a flip. Each question the move asks is answered as the CPU would (`resolveSearch`, guessing the player's too), and the position after it is scored from what the CPU can see (`positionScore`: Prize cards first, then each side's Pokémon by HP left, stage, Energy, how ready their attacks are and Special Conditions, Pokémon Tools, the hands' sizes, its own by how useful the cards are, and a deck running out; a move that ends the turn costs the attack). After Develop it plays any such Trainer, power or Stadium that improves that score; in Energy, a special Energy card where it does better than the attachment rule's pick (`specialAttachment`); in Boost, any such Trainer that makes this turn's attack better (Technical Machines, Recall, Pokémon Reversal…: `attackBoost`) or keeps the Active Pokémon from being Knocked Out next turn (`survive`). Cards with rules of their own are left to those rules. What the score can't see, it doesn't pay for: cards that only show information or rearrange a deck (Pokédex, Oracle, Radio Tower), and Stadiums or lasting effects that help neither side's attacks this turn or next, are rarely played. A self-play check (every Trainer, special Energy and Pokémon with a power in both decks, 776 games) found no illegal moves. The attack it picks can be any it can use, Technical Machines', Recall's and Psymimic's included (`situationAttacks`). Random decks follow the difficulty (`generateDeck`'s `difficulty`): a Trainer CPU's hold only cards it plays; a gym leader's (the Ace trainers' and Training at Gym leader) add disruption (`gymLeaderExtra`), Pokémon Breeder, owners' Trainers and more special Energy (`gymLeaderSpecial`).

Every difficulty answers the choices other cards ask of it: every difficulty draws 5 for Computer Error, takes an Eevee evolution for Chain Reaction, puts back the card it wants least for Special Delivery, and keeps the Energy that leaves Suicune closest to attacking for Pure Body.

It only uses what the player could see. What an attack would do is worked out in `cpu/shared.ts` by playing it on copies of the state with fixed coin flips covering every heads/tails pattern of three flips, so it never knows the real flips to come; "Knocks Out" means in at least half of those outcomes. The player's threat is their active's best attack next turn with the Energy it has now. Trainers and powers are recognised by name, so reprints work the same, and nothing depends on the current test decks.

### Seeing the CPU's moves

The CPU's reply plays back one move at a time (`game.act` returns the view after each). Each move that plays or uses a card comes with a highlight (`moveHighlights`, `packages/game-engine/src/highlight.ts`): the card (a Trainer, an Energy attached, a Pokémon benched or evolved, the Pokémon whose power it used, the Stadium, Clefairy Doll) and the Pokémon in play, either side's, that the move changed. The board shows the card large (`PlayedCard`) on a radial shadow, over the CPU's side between its Bench and Active at 1.4 times the Active's size on a big screen (agreed), in the middle of the play area on a phone, as TCG Pocket does: it pops in, is held, then bursts away (a white flare, a ring of light and sparks); then those Pokémon glow white once (Slot's `spotlight`); such a move stays on screen 1.42s instead of 0.9s (the card held still from 0.3s to 1s). A card move with coin flips shows its card first; the coins spin once it has burst away, and then the move's result is shown. Pokémon don't glow after a move whose coin flips were played out (agreed); with "Skip coin flip animations" on, they glow as after any other move. The answer to a question its own card asked (a Pokémon Power's, a Trainer's) shows no card, only the Pokémon it changed glowing (the card was shown once, when it was played or used). Attacks, retreats, ending the turn and the setup show no card. The side panel's Options tab can turn off showing the CPU's Pokémon cards (benched, evolved, or whose power it used) and Energy cards (`useOptions`); their Pokémon still glow, at once, and the move takes the usual 0.9s. Trainer cards (Stadiums and Clefairy Doll included) are always shown. When a turn begins, a banner (`TurnBanner`) slides across the middle of the play area from right to left, saying whose turn it is and the turn count (both players' turns counted together); the next move waits for it (1.44s). A game opened mid-turn shows none.

## Deck builder

The **Collection** tab (`pages/Decks.tsx`) builds decks for runs and single battles. It opens on the deck list: a button per deck showing its name, card count, its 3 headline Pokémon and how its cards split between Pokémon, Trainers and Energy. The headline Pokémon are the highest stages, preferring an evolved Pokémon with 2 or more copies of each earlier stage in the deck, then the most copies, one per evolution line while there are enough lines. From the list you can start a new deck, rename one with the pencil button by its name (Enter or clicking away saves the name, Escape cancels), copy one (the copy, "<name> (copy)", goes at the end and opens for editing), or drag decks into a new order (`Deck.position`, which Play's deck pickers follow too). Click a deck to open it: it stays at the top as a button back to the list, where its pencil button renames it (saved with the deck's Save, like its cards), with Save and Delete and its cards below, where − and + change the counts; click cards in the search to add copies. Right-click any card (in the search, the open deck, or a deck's headline Pokémon) to see it large, as holding it does; it stays open until Escape or a click outside, and with a deck open it has − and + to change the card's copies in it. Decks are saved per user (`Deck`, `DeckCard`) through the `decks` router (`apps/api/src/routers/decks.ts`: `list`, `get`, `save`, `rename`, `duplicate`, `reorder`, `delete`, and `exportCode`/`importCode` for [Deck sharing](#deck-sharing)).

The rules (`packages/shared/src/deck.ts`, used by both the builder and the API) are the rulebook's: exactly 60 cards; at most 4 cards with exactly the same name, across all sets, except basic Energy (TCGdex "Normal" Energy; Double Colorless, Darkness and Metal Energy are special); at most 4 Basic Pokémon with Unown in their names, whatever their letters (printed on every Unown: `MAX_UNOWN`, `unownCount`; the random decks take one Unown at most); at most 1 of each Shining Pokémon and 1 Miracle Energy (printed on them: `onlyOnePerDeck`, `maxCopiesOf`; the random decks take no Shining Pokémon); and at least one Basic Pokémon (Baby Pokémon count; Clefairy Doll and Mysterious Fossil don't). An unfinished deck can be saved, but never one over 60 cards, with more than 4 of a name or with more than 4 Unown, and only a deck with no problems can start a run (`run.start`) or a single battle (`game.create`).

Only a deck of unlocked cards can be played (see [Runs](#runs)): the starter deck's cards, every card gained in a run, and basic Energy, which always is. Locked cards are darker on the Collection tab, and shown still, without the holo effect, when held or right-clicked. They can still go in a deck (agreed with the user, for imported decks): adding one shows a warning, the deck lists "Holds cards you haven't unlocked" as a problem (`hasLockedCards`, `LOCKED_DECK_PROBLEM`; the API's `decks.list` and `run.overview` say `locked`), and it's greyed out, tagged Locked, wherever a deck is picked. The API saves such a deck (`checkCards` in `routers/decks.ts` checks only the size, 4-of-a-name and Unown rules), but won't start a run or battle with it (`playableDeck`).

Every catalog card can go in a deck. `cards.list` marks the ones the engine fully implements (`isImplemented` in the campaign's `card-fixes.ts`, through `apps/api/src/cards.ts`: every attack with text, its power, its Trainer effect, or basic, Double Colorless, Rainbow, Full Heal and Potion Energy), for the Works and Not work filters and the deck list's "(partial)". The rest still load: their Pokémon attack for base damage, while their other attack effects, powers, Trainer effects (the card can't be played) and special Energy effects (1 Energy of its type, or Colorless) do nothing yet.

The kind toggles (Pokémon, Trainer, Energy) show any of the kinds that are on; with none on, every kind shows, so Trainer and Energy together show every Trainer and Energy card. The type toggles pick types for Pokémon (their type) and Energy (the type it provides; special Energy without one of its own, like Double Colorless, is Colorless) alike. The type toggles and the Pokémon filters (stage, HP, has ability) only say something about the cards they apply to: the cards they don't apply to (Trainers for the types; Trainers and Energy for the Pokémon filters) still show when their kind is on, and are hidden otherwise. So Fire alone shows Fire Pokémon and Fire Energy, and Fire with Pokémon and Trainer on shows Fire Pokémon and every Trainer (`filterForDeck`).

## Deck sharing

Decks can be shared as codes and published for everyone (agreed with the user):

- **Share codes** (`apps/api/src/deck-codes.ts`): a deck's name and cards, deflated and base64url-encoded behind `PTCG1.` (a 60-card deck's is about 230 characters). The open deck's Share button (a saved deck, its changes saved) shows its code to copy (`decks.exportCode`); the deck list's Import button pastes one (`decks.importCode`), which is saved as a new deck at the end of the list and opened. Spaces and line breaks from pasting are ignored.
- **Text deck lists** (`apps/api/src/deck-text.ts`): Import also takes a deck list as decklist sites write it, a line per card ("4 Lickitung JU 38": copies, name, set code, number; "4x" and the online game's "* " work too), section headers like "Pokémon: 15" skipped. Set codes are Limitless's and the online game's (`SET_CODES`: BS, JU, FO, BS2/B2, TR, WP/PR, G1/GH, G2/GC, N1–N4, SI, LC, EX, AQ, SK) or our TCGdex ids. A line is matched by set and number when the name there matches, otherwise by name in that set; without a set code, the name's earliest print (`resolveDeckText`; names compare without accents, case or punctuation). A line no card matches is named in the error and nothing is saved. It's then imported as a code would be (named "Imported deck", holos as above).
- **Holos**: a code (and a community deck) lists each card's non-holo version when it has one (`plainCards`: a special holo's twin from `holoTwins`, a holo Energy's basic Energy), so it doesn't say which holos its author pulled. Importing puts back as many of the importer's own holos as they have of each (`withOwnHolos`, the most-pulled holo Energy style first) and fills the rest with the non-holo version.
- **Locked cards**: an import keeps the cards the importer hasn't unlocked (`importDeck` in `routers/decks.ts`); the deck just can't be played until they are (see [Deck builder](#deck-builder)), and the deck builder says so when it opens.
- **Community Decks** (`pages/Community.tsx`, `routers/community.ts`; `CommunityDeck`, `CommunityDeckCard`, migration `20261029090000_community_decks`): the Share dialog's Publish puts a snapshot of the deck on the Community Decks tab with an optional description (`community.publish`; only a deck that follows the rules, locked cards or not). Later changes to the deck don't change it; publishing again adds another. Every account sees the latest 200 (`community.list`) with the author's account name (never their Google name or email; see below), and can import one (`community.import`, as with a code) or copy its code. A tile shows its 3 headline Pokémon large (`DeckSummary` `large`) with the Pokémon/Trainer/Energy bar under them and their names, on one line with commas, over the description on their right (under them on a narrow tile), and how many of its cards you haven't unlocked only when there are some. Each tile is tagged, top right before Yours and the card count, with the generations its cards are from (G1, G2…, by their sets in `GENERATIONS`; basic Energy not counted), and a Generation dropdown and the search beside the account name (`GenerationFilter` with `toggles`, all on to start) shows the decks with cards from any generation ticked. Authors can unpublish their own (`community.unpublish`).
- **Account name** (`User.accountName`, migration `20261030090000_account_names`): the name a player shares decks under, chosen from the header's account menu, the Community Decks tab or the Share dialog (`AccountNameField`; `community.accountName`, `community.setAccountName`): 2–20 letters, numbers, spaces and `. _ ' -`, not unique (anyone may use any name). Publishing needs one. Changing it changes the name on the account's published decks. The Google profile's name (`User.displayName`, copied at each sign-in) is never shown to other players.

## Phone layout

On a phone held upright (`useIsPhone`) three screens get their own layout; tablets, desktops and phones on their side keep the usual ones.

- **The battle screen** (`Board` with `components/phone/`) fills the screen over the site's header and is locked in place (`useLockedScreen`, and the board is `touch-none`): it doesn't scroll, bounce or zoom. It's laid out like TCG Pocket (`PhoneTable`; agreed 2026-10-23: no line at the top saying who the CPU is, its items or its hand size, its hand shown face down in a strip at the top above its Bench instead; both Active Pokémon the same, smaller size (1.36–1.68 Bench widths); the piles right of each Active), the cards sized to fit the screen (`.phone-cards`; tuned for 2340×1080 phones, 360×780 CSS pixels, with or without the browser's bars: the Active Pokémon grow into the height tall screens have to spare). Tap your Active Pokémon for its moves, in a panel on the card as on a big screen (`cardPanel`, agreed), and a benched Pokémon with a power for that power's; the panel steps aside while a move needs one of your Pokémon tapped (its question showing as the prompt; an attack that takes several of the CPU's Benched Pokémon, Tunneling, is also confirmed with the Attack button in the bar), and closes once the move is made. At either end of the Bench it lines up with the card's outer edge, to stay on the screen. The layout keeps room in the bar for the Stadium's height whether or not one is in play, so your Bench never slips under the hand. Your benched Pokémon's powers, the Stadium and the hand work as on the big screen (the Stadium 1.3 times the piles' size, centred in the bar with its other things); the hand's cards are smaller, 70% of each showing above the edge (agreed); a tap brings one up whole and 1.5 times larger, until a tap anywhere else (or on it again), and selects it as a click does. A tap on a hand card selects it as a click does. Holding a finger on one zooms in on it, large above the hand; sliding along the hand zooms in on each card the finger passes, and sliding up off the hand picks up the zoomed card to drag into play (a card that can't be played says why instead). Letting go in the hand just closes the zoom: a hand card never opens the full-screen card view on a phone (agreed), though cards in play still do (`Hand`, `compact`). The round ☰ button opens a full-screen menu (`LogSheet` in `LogDrawer.tsx`): the site's pages at the top (`PageMenu`, a row of icon-only buttons; the battle screen covers the header), then the side panel's tabs (Run, Battle log, Options, Debug) below, with Concede. Who you're up against and the battle's rules are the top line, in place of the banner. There's no hover preview: a long press on any card inspects it (`useTouchHold`; the `CardInspector` then ignores the finger lifting).
- **The Collection tab** fills the screen with the cards (narrower columns), under a bar with two buttons and the number of cards shown. **Decks** (or a swipe to the right anywhere on the page) slides in the deck list (smaller tiles here: name, 3 headline Pokémon and a thin Pokémon/Trainer/Energy bar under them, its counts as the bar's tooltip) or the open deck from the left; **Filters** (with how many are set) slides in the search from the right (`SidePanel`), each filter or pair of related ones on its own line under its name. Opening a saved deck keeps the panel open, showing that deck's cards (agreed); starting a new one closes it, so cards can be tapped into it.
- **The Packs tab** shows the packs in a grid 4 wide (Kanto's 8 sets are 2 rows), each smaller, with its name and how much of it is unlocked; a tap on a locked pack says under the grid how to unlock it.

On a phone the site menu is the top bar's ☰ panel (see [Site menu](#site-menu)).

## Debug tools

A game has a **Debug** tab for testing (on a big screen beside the Run log and Battle log tabs in the site menu, `NavRail`; on a phone in the battle's ☰ menu): show a test coin flip, random, heads or tails, in the player's coin (`requestTestFlip`; only in the browser: nothing is sent and the game doesn't change, shown whatever the skip setting), change the CPU's difficulty, draw a card, put any card (or an Energy) into your hand, set the HP of any Pokémon in play, and turn Special Conditions (Asleep, Confused, Paralyzed, Poisoned, Burned) on or off on either active, for example to try Burned before any card inflicts it. It only exists outside production, enforced in two places:

- **API** (the authority): the `debug.run` procedure is a `debugProcedure` (`apps/api/src/trpc.ts`), which answers `NOT_FOUND` unless `env.debugEnabled` (`apps/api/src/env.ts`). That's always false in production (`VERCEL_ENV=production` or `NODE_ENV=production`, whatever else is set), and true elsewhere unless `ENABLE_DEBUG=false`. `debug.enabled` tells the client which.
- **Web**: `__DEBUG_BUILD__` is a build-time constant from `vite.config.ts`: on in the dev server (unless `VITE_ENABLE_DEBUG=false`), off in a `vite build` (unless `VITE_ENABLE_DEBUG=true`). A production build leaves the Debug tab's code out of the bundle entirely; the tab also needs `debug.enabled` from the API.

The pack window has two more, **Debug: force holo Energy** and **Debug: force all-rares pack**: every pack opened gets a holo Energy, or is all rares (for the fireworks), for testing them (`packs.debugOpen`, a `debugProcedure`; the checkbox is behind `__DEBUG_BUILD__` and the API's debug switch like the Debug tab).

In debug mode the run picker also unlocks every run type, the Gym Challenges and Champion runs included (started with `run.debugStart`, a `debugProcedure`; the picker's override is behind `__DEBUG_BUILD__` and the API's debug switch).

During a run, each battle in the next-encounter panel has a **Skip battle** button under its Battle (or Challenge) button: it chooses the battle (with the deck picked, an Ace Trainer's accepted) and wins it at once without opening the game, with no ₱, EXP or card reward, the run going straight on to the next step (`run.debugSkipBattle`, a `debugProcedure`: the battle's game is ended with the engine's `WIN` debug command, saved as a `DEBUG` action row, then settled as a won battle with `skipped` on `BATTLE_OVER`; the button is behind `__DEBUG_BUILD__` and the API's debug switch).

Debug commands go through the engine's `applyDebug` (`packages/game-engine/src/debug.ts`), are written to the game log as "Debug: …", and are saved in the `GameAction` log as `{ type: 'DEBUG', command }`.

## Setup

Requires Node 24+, pnpm, and Docker (Docker Desktop on Windows and macOS) for the local Supabase that development runs on by default (Option B; `pnpm dev --remote` runs on the hosted project without it).

Windows: Docker Desktop turns on Hyper-V/WSL, whose WinNAT reserves blocks of the dynamic port range at each boot, and a reserved port can't be listened on (Vite fails with `listen EACCES: permission denied ::1:5173`). On a machine whose dynamic range was moved down from the default 49152–65535 (`netsh int ipv4 show dynamicport tcp`), those blocks can cover 5173 and 8787; in the default range they can still cover the local Supabase's 5432x ports. `pnpm dev` and `pnpm db:local` check first (`reservedPorts` in `scripts/supabase-env.mjs`, reading `netsh interface ipv4 show excludedportrange protocol=tcp`) and print the fix: in an administrator PowerShell, `netsh int ipv4 set dynamic tcp start=49152 num=16384` and the same for `ipv6` (only for a moved-down range), then `net stop winnat` and `net start winnat` (or reboot).

```sh
pnpm install
cp .env.example .env        # fill in values (see below)
pnpm db:migrate             # create tables (prompts for a migration name)
pnpm db:seed                # load the card catalog (WOTC era and EX sets, 1999–2007) from the TCGdex snapshot, plus the hand-made cards
                            # (TCGdex's mistakes are corrected on the way in: TCGDEX_FIXES in prisma/seed.ts, e.g. promo Jigglypuff's 50 HP)
pnpm dev                    # web on :5173 and API on :8787, both with live reload (asks which checkout first when there are worktrees, then which database: local by default)
```

### Option A – hosted Supabase project

1. Copy the pooled (6543) and direct (5432) connection strings into `DATABASE_URL` and `DIRECT_URL`. They're under **Project Settings → Database**.
2. Copy the project URL and anon key into `SUPABASE_URL`, `VITE_SUPABASE_URL` and `VITE_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY`.
3. Enable the Google provider under **Authentication → Providers**.
4. Under **Authentication → URL Configuration**, add `http://localhost:5173` and your Vercel URL to the redirect URLs.

### Option B – local Supabase (Docker; the default for development)

```sh
pnpm db:local               # starts the stack, points .env at it, migrates, seeds the catalog when it's empty
pnpm db:local --remote      # puts the hosted values back (from .env.remote)
```

Dev servers, worktrees and review jobs should use the local stack, not the hosted project: every API restart (`tsx watch` restarts on each save) reloads the whole card catalog, and on the hosted project that's egress (about 4 MB a restart; it was most of the project's egress in October 2026). `pnpm dev` asks which database to run on, the local Supabase (the default, first in the list) or the hosted project (`pnpm dev --local` / `--remote` skip the question; a shell that isn't a terminal gets the local one). Local starts the stack if it isn't running, applies the checkout's migrations and seeds an empty catalog (`scripts/supabase-env.mjs`); hosted takes its values from `.env.remote` (or `.env`, while it still points at the hosted project). The choice reaches the servers as environment variables (`turbo.json`'s `globalEnv` passes them through), which win over `.env` (the API's dotenv, Vite and Prisma never override them), so `.env` is left as it is. `pnpm db:local` is for what reads `.env` itself (Prisma Studio, the seed, throwaway `_*.tmp.ts` scripts): `scripts/local-db.mjs` saves the hosted `.env` to `.env.remote` the first time, then writes the local values: the DB URL (`127.0.0.1:54322`) as `DATABASE_URL` and `DIRECT_URL`, `http://127.0.0.1:54321` as `SUPABASE_URL` and `VITE_SUPABASE_URL`, the local anon key, and an empty `SUPABASE_JWT_SECRET` (the local stack signs with ES256 and serves a JWKS, as the hosted one does). Worktrees copy the main checkout's `.env` (`pnpm dev`), so they follow it, and they all share the one local stack (Studio on http://127.0.0.1:54323). The local database starts with no accounts: sign in again for a new one. Google sign-in needs the local redirect URI (see Google OAuth). `pnpm supabase:stop` stops the stack and keeps its data.

### Google OAuth

1. In Google Cloud Console, create an OAuth client ID of type *Web application*.
2. Add this authorized redirect URI: `https://<project-ref>.supabase.co/auth/v1/callback`. For local Supabase use `http://127.0.0.1:54321/auth/v1/callback`.
3. Paste the client ID and secret into the Supabase dashboard. For local Supabase, set `SUPABASE_AUTH_EXTERNAL_GOOGLE_CLIENT_ID` and `SUPABASE_AUTH_EXTERNAL_GOOGLE_SECRET` in `.env`.

## Deploying to Vercel

1. Import the repo into Vercel and leave the **Root Directory** as the repo root. `vercel.json` sets the build command, which writes `.vercel/output` (Build Output API): the web app as static files and the API bundled into one function serving `/api/*`.
2. Add the variables from `.env.example` in the Vercel project settings. Use the **pooled** `DATABASE_URL`.
3. Run migrations against production from your machine or CI with `pnpm --filter @poke-tcg/db migrate:deploy`. They don't run during the Vercel build, so preview deploys can't touch the production schema.

## Scripts

| Command | Description |
|---|---|
| `pnpm dev` | Run all apps with live reload. With worktrees (`.claude/worktrees/`), it first asks which checkout to run, the main one or a worktree (↑/↓ or a number, Enter; Esc quits; `scripts/dev.mjs`); a worktree with no `.env` gets a copy of the main checkout's, one with no `node_modules` a `pnpm install`. Then it asks which database: the local Supabase (the default; started, migrated and seeded as needed) or the hosted project (`.env.remote`); `--local` / `--remote` skip the question (see Setup, Option B). Starting up shows a progress bar instead of the usual logs (a step that fails prints its output and stops; errors from the servers are printed as they come); once both servers are up the screen is cleared to the web link and a running list of errors labelled `[frontend]` or `[backend]` (with the lines that follow them, such as a stack trace). `pnpm dev --verbose`, or a shell that isn't a terminal, shows every log as before |
| `pnpm dev:here` (or `pnpm dev --here`) | Run all apps in this checkout without asking |
| `pnpm build` | Build everything |
| `pnpm typecheck` | Typecheck all packages |
| `pnpm test` | Run Vitest suites (game engine, campaign) |
| `pnpm db:migrate` / `db:studio` / `db:seed` | Prisma migrate dev / Studio / seed |
| `pnpm db:local` (`--remote`) | Point `.env` at a local Supabase, started, migrated and seeded (or back at the hosted project; `scripts/local-db.mjs`, see Setup) |
| `npx tsx scripts/generate-ex-templates.ts` (in `packages/game-engine`) | Rewrite `src/effects/ex-template-data.ts`: the EX attacks played by a shared wording (see Shared EX wordings) |
| `pnpm cards:fetch` | Re-download the card snapshot (the WOTC era and Generation 3's EX sets) from TCGdex (`packages/db/prisma/data/tcgdex-wotc.json`). Cards TCGdex doesn't have are kept by hand in `custom-cards.json` beside it (same shape; `pnpm db:seed` loads both), with their images in `apps/web/public/cards/<id>/` as `high.webp` and `low.webp` |

## Performance

What keeps the game quick, and the measurements behind it (three whole gym leader vs gym leader games on random decks, 367 CPU decisions, Node 24):

- **One card library per game.** `cloneState` (engine.ts) copies a state for a change but its `cards`: nothing changes the library after `createGame` (`applyDebug` replaces it with a new object), and copying its definitions was most of the cost of every action and of every move the CPU tries on a copy (`cpu/shared.ts`, `cpu/evaluate.ts`). A test plays a game on a frozen library. 19.1 → 13.1 ms per CPU decision.
- **One attack simulation per state per decision.** `attackOptions` plays every attack over every coin-flip pattern; a gym leader decision asked it of the same state at nearly every step (retreat, heal, disrupt, boost, tickle, attack, the generic judgement's attack loss). Each CPU's decision runs inside `withAttackMemo`, which remembers the answers by state for its length (never longer: a state is never changed while a decision runs, but tests change states in place between runs). 13.1 → 9.1 ms per decision, the same moves as before.
- **The view's damage ranges once per state.** `getPlayerView`'s `attackDamage` (view.ts) is kept by state, for a state viewed more than once.
- **The web app in chunks.** Each page is its own chunk (`lazyRouteComponent` in main.tsx), as are the pack window, the audio player, the card inspector with the holo styles, and the rules engine, which only the battle page loads (`typeName` lives in the engine's `cards.ts`, not `attacks.ts`, so the campaign's labels don't pull the rules in with it); the workspace packages are `sideEffects: false`, so unused modules are dropped. The first load went from one 2,156 kB script (580 kB gzipped) to about 920 kB over the preloaded chunks, the 700 kB engine chunk among the rest only in a battle. The campaign's route maps (190 kB, 28 kB gzipped) still load with the first page: the run types hold them, and the music follows them.
- **The catalog bundled with the API.** The Vercel build reads the Card table once (`apps/api/scripts/dump-catalog.ts`, run by `vercel-build.mjs`) and puts it beside the function as `catalog.json` (6,120 cards, 5.4 MB), with an md5 fingerprint of the table worked out in the database. A new instance (`src/catalog.ts`) asks the database only for the fingerprint, a single short row, and uses the bundled copy while it matches; after a re-seed it doesn't, and the instance reads the table itself (logging a warning) until the next deploy. Without the file (the dev servers, or a build that couldn't reach the database) it reads the table. Every card lookup is served from it, in the database's id order (the order the run pools were always built in): `catalogRows`, `holoTwins`, the run pools (`pool.ts`), the packs' sets and Energy, card libraries for games (`loadCardLibrary`), deck checks, crafting, favourites and unlocks. The hosted database's egress was mostly whole-catalog reads (October 2026: about 1,800 of them in 11 days, some 5 GB).
- **The catalog once per API instance.** `catalogRows` (`apps/api/src/cards.ts`) loads the catalog once, and `holoTwins` (unlocks.ts) is worked out from it rather than from a second read of the whole Card table (that read doubled the catalog's egress, about 4 MB more on each instance's first requests and each dev server restart); `cards.list` builds its list from it once and is served with `Cache-Control: public, max-age=3600` (app.ts; only when asked for on its own, which the web app's tRPC client does: `splitLink` in `lib/trpc.ts` sends it as a lone GET with no Authorization header, never in a batch with the per-user queries fired in the same tick, so a reload takes the 2.66 MB catalog (166 kB gzipped) from the browser's cache; issue #112), and text deck imports (`deck-text.ts`) match names against it instead of querying every card. `packs.list`'s cold start loads the Energy cards and the sets' release dates once for every set's `packEnergy` instead of per set (about 100 queries → 35).
- **The Collection's grid in view only (issue #112).** The catalog is 5,160 cards (1,712 WOTC, 1,694 EX, 1,514 EX reverse holos, 240 holo Energy), and every one was a cell of about 4 elements (some 20,000), all reconciled again on every pointer move of the decks pane's handle, every keystroke in the deck's name and every copy added. Now `CardGrid` renders only the rows that reach the viewport (`gridWindow`: a card hangs over the next rows, so the window starts at the first row whose card still reaches it), under 200 cells on a 1080p screen (about 19 rows of 9); the grid and its cells (`GridCell`, taking numbers and flags, not the counts map or `isLocked`) are memoised, with the page's handlers kept the same between renders (`addCard`/`removeCard` in Decks.tsx, `useHoldToInspect`'s `press`/`clicked`, `useInspectOnRightClick`), so a pane drag or a rename renders no cell and a copy added renders one. The search folds each card's name and attacks once (`matchesSearch`): 3.5 → 0.4 ms a search over the whole catalog (measured for #112 with the text folded beforehand). Filtering (3.5 ms) and sorting (0.1 ms) were never the cost. Not measured in a browser yet: a Chrome Performance recording of a pane drag and a fast scroll would show it.
- **Fewer renders and lookups.** The deck builder's grid follows the filters a beat behind the typing (`useDeferredValue`); the route map places its figures again only when they change, not on every walk frame, is moved directly while dragged, keys its figures by spot so a step's change doesn't remount them, and fetches the player sprites when it first shows; the board warms each card's large image once. A run action builds the pool's index once; `generateDeck` indexes the pool's Pokémon and Trainers instead of searching it per card; the seed's `stageOf` looks stages up in a map.

## Next steps

- Card effects for sets after the Base Set: Jungle and Fossil are done; later sets next. The gym leader CPU plays every card (hand-written rules, then its generic judgement); the Trainer CPU its short list.
- Runs: more run types and gym leaders, item balance, richer encounters. Remove the Retry with 3 HP testing button before release.
- Multiple saves per account; runs through later generations.
